# Dockhand roadmap

This is the source of truth for what gets built next and in what order. [Design v3](design-v3.md) defines behavior, [architecture](architecture.md) maps it onto the code, and [usage](usage.md) is the guide. What was decided, and what was declined, is in [decisions](decisions.md); what each release carries is in its notes in [releases](releases/); what was done, and why, is in Git's history, and the documents this roadmap once linked are at tag `docs-archive-2026-10-25`. A done entry cites its commits and leaves once its release's candidate is cut, the release's notes carrying it from there and the commit that removes it saying so. Implementation writes its own done entries, with their commits and measures, on main; prioritization places new work and coalesces, starting each placement from main's roadmap (the person's "Do #3, #5, #2 & #1", 2026-10-31 07:45Z, on the coordinator's process list). Every list meant for implementation, a review's, field testing's, or a session's notes, is folded in with a coalescing run across the roadmap before it's built (the person, 2026-10-19 18:45Z and 18:47Z).

## Where dockhand stands

The whole loop is built and used for real work, MacPorts pull requests and dockhand's own port among them. On main:

- **Branches:** `setup`, `start`, `adopt` (`--pr` for someone's pull request), `path`, `archive`, `clean`, and `undo`.
- **Authoring:** `update`, `create`, `checksums`, `revbump`, and `edit`; `ship` updates, checks, and submits, and `bump` does it asking nothing.
- **Understanding:** `status`, `outdated`, `diff`, `impact`, `explain`, `config`, and `open`.
- **Checks:** `check` in Tart (macOS 12 through 27, each with an Xcode image), on the fork's GitHub Actions, through a command of your own, or in a MacPorts prefix on this Mac (`local`); `logs`, `retry`, `shell`, `artifacts`, `wait`, and `cancel`.
- **Review and submit:** `tidy`, `rebase`, `submit`, `review`, and `decide`; `serve` runs queued checks and the daily look at the selected ports.

v0.6.0 is the latest release (2026-10-29, b89e8ca1, the commit of rc8, its GitHub Release published). v0.7.0's rc1 is tagged on 7017fbf8 (2026-10-30), at the person's word, its acceptance on the M1 taking the idle serve's profile and A7's look in its own B7 and A7. Its rc2 is tagged on bca3ea1f (2026-10-31 03:19Z), at the person's word, carrying the fixes found since rc1 (the person's "rc2 anyway", 2026-10-30), and its acceptance runs on the M1. Its rc3, carrying the three fixes found since rc2 (the person's "Do the rc3", 2026-10-31 03:47Z), is tagged on 880484c9 (2026-10-31 04:23Z), and v0.8.0's work reaches main from here; its own pass is skipped, its rows running on rc4 (the person's "Skip rc3; all on rc4.", 13:23Z). rc4 is tagged on 8ee049fe (2026-10-31 13:29Z), `release/v0.7`, rc3 with what's found since, and rc5 on 8edca8dc (14:42Z), rc4 with `clean --delete`'s kept edits; rc6, rc5 with `clean --delete` removing only what dockhand made (the person's "rc6 already? Let's do it, I suppose.", 14:50Z), takes the pass from its tag, rc2's full stage stopping at the end of the row it's on, and rc4 and rc5 running nothing of their own (his "switch at rc6's tag, rc6 runs the touched and unreached rows", 14:58Z, in Prime-time).

From Codex's workflow review of 2ad896d2 (the architecture thread's assessment of 2026-10-28, every gap holding on main ea5bf52d; the person's "Have prioritization process the rest as per your recommendations.", 2026-10-28 20:02Z), in this order. The workflow is sound, and each gap sits where one activity hands its result to another, so each fix goes at the boundary that owns it, then an agreement check across the direct commands, ship, bump, serve, and review, with no new driver and no split for size alone. Codex's probes become tests at their owners, renamed, the six that assert a gap inverted. W3 to W8 are in v0.6.0's code too, W3 a known issue in its notes; W2 and D1 are v0.7.0's own:

- **`review --check` checks the commit it reviewed** (W2; done 2026-10-29, 5583496c). `Review` fetches the pull request's head (engine/review.go), but `checkPullRequest` (command/review/reviewpr.go) passes only its number on, and `AdoptPullRequest` (engine/branches.go) returns a branch already tracked without fetching, so the check captures the local branch's HEAD: after the author pushes B over an adopted A, one JSON holds a review of B and a pass of A, exit 0. Adoption returns the head it stands for, and `review --check` checks `report.Head`: an adopted branch still at the head it was adopted from is advanced to the fetched one, and one with local commits beyond it is refused, naming both commits and suggesting `check`; the capture names the commit, not "the branch's HEAD". The first adoption's race, a push between the review and the adoption, closes the same way. It's in the trust rule's attended half (item 12, ee4a792c). As done, `AdoptPullRequest` adopts at the commit `Review` fetched, and `RefreshAdopted` moves an adopted branch with no commit or edit of its own since its last push to the new head by a reset, recording it as the last push, one with work of its own refused naming both commits and `check -b`.
- **`submit --draft` makes the pull request a draft** (W3; done 2026-10-29, 549430a1, through the GitHub CLI where an organization refuses dockhand's app, as `--ready` is). A draft request lets a failing or unchecked tree through (engine/submit.go), but only a new pull request is made a draft: an existing one gets `forge.Update`, which edits its title and body alone (forge/github/pullrequests.go), and the record keeps its old draft state, so failing work reaches a ready pull request, which design v3's publication rule forbids. The forge gains `MarkDraft` beside `MarkReady`, GraphQL's `convertPullRequestToDraft`; `submit --draft` on a ready pull request converts it before it pushes, and the preview says so; the record keeps the draft state asked for apart from the one observed. Until it lands, `--draft` on a ready pull request is refused, naming the pull request and `gh pr ready --undo`.
- **A check follows a running one only where it covers each cell** (W4, with Codex's finding 3 of 8e314b96, whose rule it is; done 2026-10-29, 21303500). The follow rule finding 3 put at the engine (614951e1, 45e3efae, and ce1a7b9a) asks `PlanCovers` (engine/evidence.go), which compares environment and target sets, tests, freshness, and variants, not each environment's build order or exclusions, and not `Keep`: `check --build all` follows a running `minmax` check with the same lists, which plans fewer cells, and `check --keep` follows a run that keeps no guest. `PlanCovers` compares cells as `coversPlan` does for finished evidence, and a kept guest is part of what a check must provide; a check it doesn't cover is refused as now, check offering `--replace`.
- **One candidate after every edit** (W5; done 2026-10-29, b03f0ddd, the evaluation and fidelity reports dropped only where a change alters what the files evaluate to, a modeline, a comment, or whitespace keeping them). `followMoves` (engine/update.go) rewrites a file's edit without rebuilding the prepared tree, and `withModelines` and `withLintFixes` (652d5e05) rebuild only when they change something, so `update --follow-moves` in a clean Portfile previews and compares the old repository and writes the new. One finalize step after every transformation rebuilds the tree once and drops the evaluated snapshot and fidelity, the three no longer rebuilding on their own, so the preview, the comparison, `editFindings`, and the files written are one candidate.
- **A description edited on GitHub after the preview isn't overwritten** (W7; done 2026-10-29, 3dd94e68, a description the person wrote whole, or one `--rewrite-description` replaces, refused where it changed). The body's merge works against the observation taken at planning (engine/submit.go), and `ApplySubmit` writes it with nothing checked. `ApplySubmit` observes again, the planned title and body its preconditions: unchanged, it writes; changed, it merges again against the fresh body where the plan allows, and otherwise refuses, naming the change. GitHub has no conditional write for a pull request's body, which the code says once; unattended, the window is nil.
- **serve resumes a branch it prepared and didn't queue** (W6; done 2026-10-29, c0d9f114, where `serve.ship_to` carries updates to a check, a branch with a pull request not one). serve's ship (engine/ship.go) updates, commits, and starts a branch before `QueueCheck` records a run, so a serve stopped between them, or a planning error after the commit, leaves a branch the next serve skips as already in it (`PlanOutdated`), with no run for the queue's recovery or the submit pass to find; `ship -b` recovers it by hand. That breaks decisions.md's "an interrupted serve never abandons work". On start, serve queues the check of each branch it owns, made by serve, open, committed, with no run and a clean worktree, and says so; one with hand edits is handed to the person. Persisting serve's intent before its side effects is the fuller shape, taken if the resume falls short.
- **A rate-limit retry keeps what it didn't look at** (W8; done 2026-10-29, 6869590a). The retry's look (engine/serve.go) is made from the retried ports and keeps only the earlier look's outdated ports before replacing outdated.json, so a port uncertain or set aside before it reads as resolved. The look keeps an observation per port, and a retry replaces only the retried ports' entries. Taken when touched's serve finding 3, the daily look off the loop, can ride it where the change is in the same code.
- **A decision on a moving source holds for the commit looked at** (D1, decided; done 2026-10-29, bd92288f, its subject `checked..current`, "jq source-moved 1a2b3c4..5d6e7f8" (model.ConcernSubject), the `--json` subject of the two concerns changing form, which v0.7.0's Upgrading says; `source-unknown` is no hold `decide` takes (D4), so it binds nothing). A decision's subject is port and rule (model/history.go), valid while the port's directory is unchanged (engine/decide.go), and the commit `source-moved` and `tag-moved` carry in their subject (engine/servesubmit.go) is dropped in matching, so an acceptance that reviewed commit B answers a later move to C. Static findings keep the directory's binding; dynamic ones, `source-moved`, `tag-moved`, and `unknown-source`, take the checked and current identity into the subject, so a new commit is a new concern, and `decide` takes the subject from the current concern, so the person's command doesn't change. It refines item 13's decisions (ce68f1a9); decisions.md has the rule.
- **`release-moved` and `assessment-moved` hold for the commits looked at** (implementation's question on D1, sent here by the coordinator on 2026-10-29 at 18:19Z; done 2026-10-29, f32f5411, status computing `assessment-moved` from the recorded assessments beside `release-moved`, so `decide` finds both as it finds `source-moved` and nothing stays bound to the directory, an assessment that recorded no commit raising none; status's moved rows drop what a decision answers, as submit's holds do). Both compare a Git-fetched port's commits, the update's release or the assessment's read against the commit the check planned (engine/servesubmit.go `preparedSources`, `assessedSources`), so a decision bound to the directory answers a later pair of commits as D1's did; each takes `<prepared>..<planned>` or `<assessed>..<planned>` as its subject, as `source-moved` does. `decide` finds `release-moved`'s commits where it finds `source-moved`'s; status doesn't compute `assessment-moved`, so `decide` takes its commits from where the hold was said, and where nothing kept them it stays bound to the directory and D1's rule in decisions.md says so.

- **One capacity contract** (Codex's review of 275125ff, finding 4's first step; one batch). Three accountings each read `e.capacity(provider)` and decide alone: `SeeAllThrough` (engine/session.go:119) reserves channel slots for its own batch, `awaitSlot` (engine/slots.go:33) counts live processes' runs before a foreground check, and serve (engine/serve.go:378) keeps `inUse` and `inFlight` for its own runs, so a batch's runs don't count serve's, nor serve's a batch's, and only `awaitSlot` sees across processes (no race reproduced; the structure makes one easy to write). One reservation, taken and released through slots.go over the store's live runs, is what every driver uses, and serve's maps go, a check that waits for it saying once what holds it wherever it waits, as rc2's line for serve's queue does (G14); the batch drive being three days old, it's cheapest now. It takes the code-organization review's serve finding 3, each worker saying a problem once and the daily look off the loop, where serve's loop is rewritten (from Taken when touched). - **Two databases on one checkout don't take each other's checkpoints** (rc2's full stage, the Prime-time session, sent here by the coordinator on 2026-10-31 at 08:41Z; not harm, a refusal that changed nothing, and in v0.6.0 too; about a fifth of a batch, inferred; done on main 2026-10-31, 826ef485: `history.Prepare` numbering past the highest name the checkout has under `refs/dockhand/checkpoints/`, the store taking a number at or above its own next; `history.KeptRefs` the one rule clean's `dropRefs` and recovery's `dropCheckpointRefs` drop by, the index ref's parent read with `git.Parents`; and `history.TakenName` the refusal, "tidy-N is another dockhand's checkpoint on this checkout, so nothing was changed; run again", read from the ref conflict). A checkpoint's ref, `refs/dockhand/checkpoints/tidy-N` or `rebase-N`, is named by its database's counter (store/sqlite `NextCheckpointNumber`, the highest recorded plus one), and a `DOCKHAND_DB` copy carries its original's counter, so a copy run on its original's checkout hands out the same names: a G13 copy made tidy-783 to 788 on dhtest's clone, dhtest's database, at 782, gave out 783 again, and tidy failed with "reference already exists". The refs move in one transaction (engine/tidy.go, `UpdateRefs` with the branch), so nothing moved. Where the names meet, a database can delete another's: clean drops every ref its merged branch's checkpoints name, whatever it holds (engine/clean.go `dropRefs`), and recovery drops a stopped checkpoint's index ref whatever it holds (history/history.go `dropCheckpointRefs`), so one database's restore point can go with another's merge. So:
  - a checkpoint's number skips a name already under `refs/dockhand/checkpoints/` in the checkout, so the name people type stays tidy-N; refs carrying the database's identity are declined, since they rename every ref a person has and need a migration for nothing a skip doesn't give;
  - clean and recovery drop a checkpoint's refs only where they hold what it kept, its `Before`, and for the index ref a commit on `Before`, as recovery already checks for the history ref;
  - where another process took the name in between, tidy and rebase say the name is another's on this checkout and nothing changed, and to run again.
  A copy run on its original's checkout still has two databases recording one clone's branches, which no naming fixes: docs/development.md's scratch runs already give a copy its own clone and worktrees, the heavy-runs rule standing on that, and the usage that names a copy says so with its archives (Chores).

- **A local guest program killed from outside dockhand isn't tried again** (rc7's G9 sitting on the M1, the Prime-time session's, sent here by the coordinator on 2026-11-02 at 17:54Z; not harm, every attempt was password-gated and each put-back restored the prefix; about a fiftieth of a batch, inferred; any time before v0.8.0's candidate). G9 killed the guest program's process group in /opt/local with kill -9. dockhand put /opt/local back, asking sudo's password past its five-minute ticket, as G9 wants, then said "provider infrastructure failed: the guest program stopped before it finished" and built again as root, three attempts, each asking the password. The local provider follows with no `Stopped` (local.go:249), so `ended` (buildenv/guest/follow.go:468–491) reads the last results, finds no finish, and returns `buildenv.Infrastructure`, a transient kind, which the runner tries again (checks/runner.go:708) without reading how the process ended: `localRun.Err()` (local.go:503) holds subprocess's error, an `exec.ExitError` whose status says signaled. A guest program that ended on a signal dockhand didn't send is no longer tried again: dockhand's own stop (`Stop`, or a canceled context) is excluded, and sudo dying on the signal itself counts too. It is marked `buildenv.ErrNeedsAttention`, so no attempt is made, and said once without that problem's "another attempt won't fix it": "the guest program in /opt/local was killed by a signal dockhand didn't send (signal: killed); a kill is someone's choice, so no other attempt was made, and dockhand check <branch> runs it again", the put-back as it is. A guest that crashed, exiting with a status and no finish, is still tried again, as is a Tart VM that stopped (follow.go:383–386, a VM's ending has more causes than a person). Asking first, Prime-time's other choice, isn't taken: serve has no terminal to ask on, and the next check is the person's to run. Tests: a fake guest killed by SIGKILL (no second attempt, the words), one that exits 1 unfinished (tried again), and dockhand's own stop (canceled, as now). - **A variant check of an unchanged port, its before and after, and the plan's evaluation printed** (field testing's curl +sectrust notes 1, 2, 8b and 7's rest, from the item above; architecture's design, /mnt/project-files/plan/variant-checks-of-unchanged-ports-2026-11-02.md, read against main f7078d59, sent here by the coordinator on 2026-11-02 at 19:45Z; confirmed here; not harm, new forms; about a seventh of a batch, architecture's; any time before v0.8.0's candidate, in this order). No branchless check: a run belongs to a branch everywhere a person reads it, and `dockhand start curl-sectrust`, the check, and `archive` hold an investigation. What field testing lacked was the no-op edit, which this removes.

A port's rows go together, its default build first, then its variant builds in the order their checks ran, newest first, each cell naming the check that built it, as earlier checks' cells do (`Built`, `sharedRuns`). A variant build beside a default build some check made is an extra, as an `--also` port is: shown where it ran, and in the ask's other environments its cell is `outsideProfile`'s exclusion, "outside what the newest check of these files asked for", never unchecked, so its absence never blocks a submission. Where no check of the files built the port's default build, every one having chosen variants, the variant builds are the port's coverage, as today when such a check is the primary. The target's role carries it: a build the primary didn't plan joins as `model.Also` where the port has a default build in the evidence, and with the primary's own role where it hasn't.
  - the newest result of a build in an environment stands, every cell's rule today: a check that fails `curl +ssl +sectrust` on tahoe after one that passed it replaces the pass, and the reverse.   - the variants item is ticked where the evidence holds a variant build of a changed port and every variant build of changed ports that stands passed wherever it ran, `--variants each` and chosen variants alike, from any check of the files.   - status reads the same evidence: a tree whose checks all passed, variant builds included, is CheckPassed; a failed variant build that stands is ExtraFailed, "Next: dockhand submit --accept 'curl +ssl +sectrust'", or a check again; an earlier check's variant build stays in the detail's table after a later defaults check. - **A check's staged tree, input.tar, doesn't outlive the attempt that staged it** (Prime-time's v0.7.0 acceptance, sent here by the coordinator on 2026-11-03 at 22:13Z: when serve resumes an interrupted check, the interrupted attempt's input.tar, 173 MB, stays in ~/.dockhand/logs/check-N/ after the check ends; not harm, disk alone; about a fiftieth of a batch, inferred; any time before v0.8.0's candidate).
  - today Tart stages the revision into `<logs>/check-N/<environment>-<attempt>/input.tar` and removes it by a `defer` when `Execute` returns (tart/provider.go:624–625). So it sits beside the log for the whole build, though the guest has its own copy once `launch` has uploaded and unpacked it (provider.go:706, 1020–1031). A process stopped mid-build (serve stopping, an upgrade's serve taking over, the Mac restarting, kill -9) runs no `defer`, and the resumed check builds in the next attempt's directory (checks/runner.go:674, 872), so the stopped attempt's archive stays. Log cleanup compresses a finished check's `.log` files only (`cleanLogs`, engine/retention.go:95; buildlog/files.go:76), so an open branch's newest check in an environment keeps the archive until its directory goes. The local provider removes its own once it has unpacked it (local/local.go:358–368), its window staging alone.
  - after, three small changes. Tart removes input.tar as soon as `launch` returns, copied into the guest or not, rather than when `Execute` ends, so a stop during the build leaves none, only one during staging or the upload. A run's end removes any `input.tar` its attempts' directories hold (`<logs>/<run>/*/input.tar`), where the runner ends it (`finish`, checks/runner.go:979), so a resumed check's stopped attempt leaves nothing once the check ends; an attempt directory left empty goes too, as runner.go:679–683 does for one with no log. `cleanLogs`, which visits each finished check's directory already, removes any input.tar it finds there, so those earlier releases left, and one under a check that ended without its driver (canceled while nothing drove it), go at the next cleanup; `LogCleanupWords` (retention.go:41) says them where there were any, "removed 2 staged trees stopped checks left, 346 MB".
  - Usage docs: none name input.tar or quote cleanup's log words, so none change.
  - Acceptance row D-I2 (tools/acceptance/rows/D-I2.sh, quick), which kills a Tart check at "building in" and resumes it with `wait`, also asserts no input.tar is left under the run's log directory once `wait` returns. Run with `tools/heavy tools/acceptance/quick.sh --rows "D-I2"` before landing.
  - Tests: Tart's input.tar gone once `launch` returns, with a fake guest, while the build goes on; a run with an earlier attempt's input.tar keeps none after `finish`, and the empty directory goes; `cleanLogs` removes one under a finished check, leaves one under a check still going, and its words count the bytes.
  - Done on main 2026-11-03, in the commit after 83f8bfca. `buildenv.StagedTree` names input.tar, and `buildenv.RemoveStagedTrees` removes every one under a check's directory, counting them and their bytes. Tart removes its staged tree as soon as `launch` returns, its `defer` kept for the paths before it; a run's `finish` removes what its attempts' directories hold and an attempt's directory left empty; `cleanLogs` removes any under a finished check before compressing its logs, `LogCleanup.Staged` and `StagedBytes` said by `LogCleanupWords` as "removed 1 staged tree stopped checks left, 2 KB". D-I2 asserts no input.tar is left under the killed check's log directory once `wait` returns. Tests: Tart's staged tree gone while the build is followed, a check ending with none left and the stopped attempt's empty directory gone, `cleanLogs` removing one under a finished check and its words, and `RemoveStagedTrees` leaving the logs. D-I2 wasn't run here: quick.sh wants the stage's Tart image, which this Mac's acceptance home lacks.

An agent's loop today is the note's: `check -d`, then `wait` in the background and a poll of its log.

Nothing `--for` follows runs in the command's own process: with `--for` the check is always serve's or a background worker's to run, so returning at the bound stops nothing.

    ```
    Next: dockhand wait check-42 --for 9m
    ```
    "building socat on macOS 26" is the journal's latest progress line, what the redrawn status line shows (`journal.show`'s `EventProgress`, `EventWaiting` and the rest); a check that hasn't started reads "check-42 is still queued after 9m, waiting for a slot on tart (check-41 is using it); it continues", the journal's last wait as it was said. The Next names the same bound. Where `wait` alone would have run the check here (nothing leads: the run is queued, or left running by a process that's gone), `wait --for` starts a background worker as `check -d` does (`Settings.Drain`) and follows it: "No dockhand serve is running; a background worker (PID 4242) runs check-42. Following it for 9m." Where the worker can't start, exit 1: "check-42 is queued and nothing runs it: Dockhand could not start a background worker (<error>). Run dockhand serve, or dockhand wait check-42 without --for runs it here". Ctrl-C before the bound: as today, 130, "stopped following check-42; it continues". `--json`: the check object `wait` emits today (branch, revision, plan, `run` with its state, `targets` empty until it finishes), and the envelope's `exit_code` 4 with `error` carrying the line above; a script reads `exit_code` and `run.state`, and nothing else is added.

A baseline `check.baseline` queues after a failure within the bound is said with its name, "check-42 · baseline queued, since check.baseline is on: dockhand wait check-43 --for 9m follows it", and left to the worker, as `-d` leaves it. One command for an agent whose tool calls are bounded: `dockhand check --for 9m`, then `dockhand wait --for 9m` until the exit code isn't 4.

  - today `dockhand rebase` (`engine.Rebase`, verbs.go:163) refuses uncommitted edits, fetches master, finds the merge base, and replays the branch's commits one by one with `git merge-tree --write-tree` (`git.Replay`), each written as an object before anything moves: a conflict names its paths and leaves the branch as it was, a merge commit is refused, a commit whose change master has is dropped; then the branch, its checkout, and a checkpoint move, "Rebased jq-1.8.1 (3 commits) from master 3f2a1c9 onto 9b8c7d6", `--json` giving branch, from, to, up_to_date, commits (a count), and checkpoint. Every other `--plan` shows what a command would do and changes nothing.
  - after: `rebase --plan` does the first half and prints it. It fetches master, as rebase and `outdated` do, which moves no branch; replays into objects nothing references, which `git gc` prunes; moves nothing and writes no checkpoint. Each commit's fate, in order:
    ```
    Rebase jq-1.8.1 (3 commits) from master 3f2a1c9 onto 9b8c7d6 (fetched just now):
      replay   a1b2c3d jq: update to 1.8.1
      drop     b2c3d4e jq: fix checksums (master 9b8c7d6 has this change)
      replay   c3d4e5f jq: whitespace
    Nothing moved. dockhand rebase replays them and keeps a checkpoint.
    ```
    A conflict stops the replay where rebase would, saying where and what's left untried: "  conflict c3d4e5f jq: whitespace, in sysutils/jq/Portfile" then "dockhand rebase would stop here, with jq-1.8.1 as it is; resolve it by hand with git rebase 9b8c7d6, or ask for help on the PR."
  - what rebase refuses, the plan says in its place, changing nothing: a branch already on master ("jq-1.8.1 already starts from master 9b8c7d6"), uncommitted edits ("jq-1.8.1 has uncommitted edits to sysutils/jq/Portfile; rebase would refuse until they're committed (dockhand tidy)"), a merge commit ("b2c3d4e is a merge commit, which dockhand doesn't replay"), and a branch master has every change of ("nothing to rebase; dockhand clean jq-1.8.1 removes it once its pull request is merged"). The notes rebase prints after moving are said as what would follow: the pull request's old commits submit would replace, and the older builds the commits' Generated-By keep.
  - `--json` keeps today's keys and adds `"plan": true` and `"replay"`, a list of `{"id", "subject", "outcome"}`, outcome `replay`, `drop`, `conflict` (with `"paths"`), or `untried`; `"commits"` stays the count replay would make.
  - implementation notes, architecture's: `git.Replay` returns what it did beside the head, `[]Replayed{Commit, Outcome, Paths}`, stopping at a conflict as now, so rebase's words and the plan's read one record; `engine.rebase` splits into `planRebase` (refusals, fetch, merge base, replay) and the move, `Rebase` calling both and `PlanRebase` the first, `Rebased` gaining `Replay []Replayed`; command/tidy/rebase.go's `--plan`, "show what the rebase would replay, and onto what, and change nothing".

- **A declared library nothing links is said, and an undeclared link names the pkg-config that brought it in** (the abook session's follow-up, 2026-10-28, and gnome-voice's note 9, 2026-10-24, from Later and Taken when touched; the person's "could make it into 0.8.0", 2026-11-04 00:19Z; architecture's design, sent here by the coordinator on 2026-11-04 at 02:31Z, /mnt/project-files/plan/declared-library-nothing-links-2026-11-04.md; not harm; about half a batch, inferred; any time before v0.8.0's candidate).
  - today the guest's link scan (guest.tcl's `links`) lists a passed target's installed files with `port contents`, reads each Mach-O's libraries with `otool -L`, and asks `port provides` whose each is, recording `model.Link{Binary, Library, Port, Declared}`; the host (`engine.linkFindings`) takes the target's `depends_lib` as `Declared`, and `evidence.UndeclaredLinks` says each port a build links and doesn't declare (`link-undeclared`, a hold), or "links openssl3 and zlib's libraries, each declared in depends_lib". `guestprogram.Protocol` is 1. Nothing reads what a declared port installs, so abook's `port:libiconv`, which nothing it built links, went unsaid.
  - the guest reads what each declared port installs: for each `depends_lib` entry as the guest's MacPorts resolves it (as `path_dependencies` reads them, `port:` and `path:` alike, by the port each names), `port -q contents <port>` once, keeping its dynamic libraries (Mach-O files under `lib/` ending `.dylib` or `.so`) and its pkg-config modules (each `.pc` file's module name, the `-l` names of its `Libs:` line, and the modules of its `Requires:` line); and it counts the Mach-O files the target installed. The result gains `declared`, a list of `{port, libraries, modules}`, and `machos`, the count; the protocol becomes 2, and a result without them, a reused older check's, is unread, said as links are today. A `port contents` that fails leaves that port out, silently.
  - the rule, `unlinked-dependency`: a passed build's declaration of port D is unlinked where all four hold: (1) the Portfile's own text declares D in a `depends_lib` option, as `portfile.portDependencies` reads it, a `port:` entry or a `path:` entry the guest resolved to D, so a dependency a PortGroup adds (python313 under python, perl5 under perl5, ruby under ruby) is never said; (2) D installs at least one dynamic library, so a port of programs alone, git or cmake, is never said; (3) the target installed at least one Mach-O, so a pure Python, Perl, or Tcl module or a script port is never said; (4) no Mach-O the target installed links a library D installs, in any environment the check built, so one linked on one release and not another is linked. A notice, never a hold: a library loaded with dlopen, or a program the port runs, declared in depends_lib by a maintainer who chose to, is theirs to keep. Its words: "declares libiconv in depends_lib, which nothing it built links: a header-only library or a build tool belongs in depends_build, and a program it runs or a library it loads at run time in depends_run". It appears beside the link line in the check's report, status's detail, and submit's preview, under the target; `check --json` carries it in the links section as `"unlinked": [{"port": "libiconv", "option": "depends_lib", "line": 23}]`; `dockhand explain unlinked-dependency` gives the rule. tao, declaring tcl and zlib and linking neither, is said, rightly.
  - the companion: for each undeclared link of port P (the hold as today), where a declared port D's modules name P's library, directly in a `Libs:` line or through `Requires:` followed module to module among the declared ports' modules, the words add how and the two ways out: "gnome-voice links cairo's library, which it doesn't declare, through gtk4's pkg-config Libs (gtk4 requires cairo); depends_lib-append port:cairo declares it, or -Wl,-dead_strip_dylibs drops what it doesn't call". The hold stands; only its words change. `--json`'s link finding gains `"through": {"port": "gtk4", "modules": ["gtk4", "cairo"]}`. A module whose `Requires:` names one no declared port installs stops there.
  - cost: `port contents` is a registry read, milliseconds a port; forty `depends_lib` entries add under a second to a build of minutes; nothing is fetched.
  - implementation notes, architecture's: guest.tcl's `declared {portdir name variants}` beside `path_dependencies`, resolving each entry with the same `mportopen`, then `contents` of each, filtered; `modules` reads `Libs:` and `Requires:` with a plain split, `${...}` left as is; `save` keeps `declared` and `machos` with `links`. `guestprogram`: `Protocol = 2`, `Result` gains `Declared []DeclaredPort` and `MachOs int`, follow.go reads them, `model.TargetResult` keeps them with `Links`. evidence/links.go: `LinkedBuild` gains `Text []portfile.PortDependency` (the Portfile's own depends_lib lines, read by the host from the revision) and the result's `Declared`; `UndeclaredLinks` returns the unlinked findings beside the undeclared and fills `Through` from the modules; engine/links.go reads the Portfile text per target directory, once. Words in command/view/results.go, status's detail, and submit's preview, where the link line is; the code in commitrules/explain.go's table.

- **A change that makes a port non-distributable holds, by MacPorts' own judge** (the Vx language port session, 2026-10-19, from Later; the person's answer, 2026-11-04 02:10Z, that MacPorts' answer is macports-infrastructure's jobs/distributable_lib.tcl; architecture's design, sent here by the coordinator on 2026-11-04 at 02:31Z, /mnt/project-files/plan/license-not-distributable-2026-11-04.md; not harm today, a missing check; about half a batch, inferred; any time before v0.8.0's candidate).
  - MacPorts' file (373 lines, BSD) is the buildbot's judge: `good_licenses`, the names it accepts, lower-case with the version stripped; `license_conflicts`, a license to those it can't be combined with (`gpl` against `openssl`, `apache-1`, `php`, …; `agpl-1` against `gpl-3`); `license_exceptions`, one entry, `opensslexception`, matched against dependency names `^openssl[0-9]*$`. `check_licenses port variants` opens the port (`infoForPort`: `mportlookup`, `mportopen`, `mportinfo`), reads `license`, `depends_build`, `depends_lib`, `installs_libs`, and `license_noconflict`, and judges: each brace group of the license must hold one known name, else "\"wasi-sdk\" is not distributable because its license \"llvm\" is not known to be distributable"; each dependency, transitively, skipping `installs_libs no` and those `license_noconflict` names, must be known and not conflict, else "… because its dependency \"openssl3\" has license \"openssl\" which conflicts with …"; an empty license, the port's or a dependency's, is not distributable.
  - today dockhand's evaluator runs port-tclsh sessions with `mportopen` on a port's directory and has no `mportlookup`; `portindex.Index` gives each port's `Fields`, `license`, `depends_build`, `depends_lib`, and `installs_libs` among them; `assess` raises `license-changed` (a hold) and `license-spdx` (a notice; macports/license.go maps SPDX names, `Apache-2.0` to `Apache-2`).
  - after: the file is kept verbatim at internal/macports/distributable/distributable_lib.tcl, header and all, and sourced in the evaluator's session by a wrapper, distributable.tcl, that redefines one proc, `infoForPort`, to answer from dockhand: the port judged from the evaluator's own `mportopen` of its Portfile, head's or base's; every dependency from the tree's port index (license, depends_build, depends_lib, installs_libs), `license_noconflict` from the index where it carries it, else from evaluating that port. `check_licenses` and the three tables run untouched, so dockhand's answer is the buildbot's. The procedure is `distributable <portdir> <subport>`, giving the Tcl's `[ok message]`, judged with default variants, as the buildbot judges. (A sources.conf naming dockhand's index, so `mportlookup` works, puts more of MacPorts' state in the evaluator for one proc's worth; a list of dockhand's own, or a fetch at run time, the person set aside.)
  - pinned by a test, watched by CI: a table test runs the wrapper over wasi-sdk's `license Apache-2 {LLVM Exception}` (not distributable, "llvm"), devel/wasmtime's `Apache-2` (distributable), a GPL-2 port depending on openssl3 (conflict), the same with `license_noconflict openssl3` (distributable), and an empty license. A weekly job, .github/workflows/distributable-master.yml beside base-master.yml, fetches jobs/distributable_lib.tcl from macports-infrastructure's master and fails where it differs from the pinned copy, naming upstream's commit: a notice for the person, not a gate, as base-master is; implementation re-pins and reruns the table.
  - when it runs, and what it says: each changed port's assessment judges head, and base where the port exists on master. The hold `not-distributable` is raised where head isn't distributable and base was, or there's no base; where neither is, a notice, holding nothing, "stays non-distributable, as on master: …". The words carry MacPorts' sentence and the line to write where dockhand knows one: "! not distributable: MacPorts' checker says "wasi-sdk" is not distributable because its license "llvm" is not known to be distributable, so the builders keep no binary archive of it. Write license Apache-2 (Cargo's Apache-2.0 WITH LLVM-exception), as devel/wasmtime does [not-distributable]". The line to write: for a group of names that are no license, the SPDX table's name for the base license where the group reads as `<license> WITH <exception>`; for a conflict, "declare license_noconflict <dependency> where the project's license exempts it, as the Guide allows; otherwise it keeps no archive"; for an empty license, "write its license"; else the reason alone. Said in update's report, status's detail, and submit's preview; it holds unattended runs as `license-changed` does; `holds.allow` and `dockhand decide not-distributable <port> --because "…"` answer it, an accepted one going into the pull request's "Reviewed:" block; `--json` carries a concern with rule `not-distributable` and MacPorts' sentence as its detail.
  - cost: a dependency closure is index reads, hundreds for a GTK port in well under a second; evaluations only for `license_noconflict` the index lacks, and the port itself, which the assessment evaluates already; nothing fetched.
  - implementation notes, architecture's: internal/macports/distributable/ holds the pinned file, distributable.tcl (the override, reading a dict the host hands in, each dependency's fields from the index), distributable.go (`Judge(ctx, session, portdir, subport, index) (ok bool, reason string)`), and the table test, both files `go:embed`ed; `eval` sources the wrapper on demand in a session, beside versions.tcl, the host passing the index's entries for the closure it walks itself (`portindex.Index` by name, transitively over depends_build and depends_lib), so the Tcl asks nothing of disk; `assess`: `NotDistributable = "not-distributable"`, judged at head and base beside `LicenseChanged`, the line to write from macports/license.go's table; commitrules/explain.go's entry; `engine.HoldRules` lists it; `decide` takes it.
  - Usage docs, same commit: docs/usage/publishing.md (line 285, the holds and `decide` examples), `not-distributable` beside `license-changed`, with the wasi-sdk words and that the builders keep no archive of such a port; docs/usage/unattended.md (line 89, the holds table), the hold, what raises it, and that `holds.allow` names it; docs/usage/configuration.md, `holds.allow`'s list of rules; docs/usage/hand-edits.md, where `license-spdx` is described, one sentence that a group of names the buildbot doesn't know makes the port non-distributable, and dockhand says so; `dockhand explain not-distributable`, MacPorts' three tables in a sentence each, and that the judge is MacPorts' own file, pinned; docs/development.md, the pin, the weekly job, and how to re-pin.

- **A running check's guest can be entered, and a failed check's clone is kept to work in** (the exiv2 session's rc4 notes and the bazel session's, 2026-10-24, from Later, with setup's last `tart exec` moved to SSH, from Taken when touched's Tart workarounds, #1346; the person's "could make it into 0.8.0", 2026-11-04 00:19Z; architecture's design, sent here by the coordinator on 2026-11-04 at 02:31Z, /mnt/project-files/plan/check-guest-to-work-in-2026-11-04.md; its two defaults the person's choices on prioritization's cards at 02:54Z, "Keep it" (keep_failed on) and "Not evidence" (an entered result); not harm; about a batch, inferred; any time before v0.8.0's candidate).
  - today `dockhand shell <check> [-- <command>]` (42f2bb1a) starts a clone a check kept with `check --keep`, reaches it over SSH with dockhand's keys (`machine.Reach`: `tart ip`, then `guestssh.Guest`), runs the command in the port's work directory, and stops the clone; `--test` reruns the test phase there, never as evidence. A clone is kept only where the guest finished and `Plan.Keep` was asked (provider.go's `keepClone`); every other is deleted as its attempt ends, and `clean` or the branch's next check removes a kept one. A running check's clone, `dockhand-check-<run>-<release>-<attempt>` (`clonePrefix`, `vmName`), is reachable only through the progress line's "building in dockhand-check-… (TART_HOME=… tart exec … …)". `tart exec` is used once more, in `provision.ReadyAgent`, setup's readiness probe.
  - a shell in a running check's guest: `dockhand shell check-12` while check-12 runs on Tart opens a shell, or runs the command, in the attempt's clone: the engine names the clone from the run's live execution and the release (`--on` chooses where several build), the provider reaches it as a check does (`Reach`, `AwaitSSH`), the shell starting in the port's work directory. Nothing is stopped afterwards; the check owns the VM. `--test` is refused there: "--test reruns the test phase in a kept clone; check-12 is running".
  - its results stay evidence only where the shell couldn't have touched them: the run records each entry, `Entered []Entry{At, Release, Command}`, and a target's result on that release counts as entered where its build hadn't finished at the entry, by the guest's step stamps: a target installed before the shell opened stands; the one building, and every one after it, is recorded as it is and marked. The report and status say "exiv2 passed on macOS 26, entered by a shell at 10:02 before its build finished"; evidence leaves such a result out as it leaves out a missing one; submit says "check-12's build of exiv2 on macOS 26 was entered before it finished, so it isn't evidence; dockhand check builds it again". Before the shell opens, one line: "check-12 is building exiv2 in dockhand-check-…-26-1; a shell here leaves exiv2's result, and any target after it, as not evidence (dockhand check again for that)." No question is asked; off a terminal, the command after `--` as today.

### v0.9.0

After v0.8.0's release: nothing here goes in before v0.8.0's candidate is cut.

- **Canary checks: `check --also a,b --set name=value` builds unchanged ports against the branch under a Portfile or PortGroup option** (the openssl4 new-port session's note 5, macports/macports-ports#36375 on v0.7.0, sent here through field testing on 2026-11-04 at 02:53Z, from Later; the person's "As shown" on prioritization's triage card at 02:58Z; architecture's design, sent here by the coordinator on 2026-11-04 at 03:14Z, /mnt/project-files/plan/canary-checks-set-2026-11-04.md, aimed at v0.9.0; not harm; about a batch, inferred).
  - the author's words: "Canary or reverse-dependency testing against a new library branch was entirely manual: start a new branch, git sparse-checkout add five dirs, cherry-pick both commits, awk `openssl.branch 4` into four Portfiles, commit, adopt. --also builds unchanged ports but can't change a setting. Something like `check --also libssh2,socat --set openssl.branch=4` would cover it, and the same need comes up for python/perl/llvm/php version moves. This clearly needs design: how settings are injected, and whether results count as evidence for the main branch." The canary branch was dockhand/openssl4-canaries, four dependents on tart:26 (check-773), openssl4's own pass reused from check-772; the linkage audit ("iperf3 links openssl4's libraries, declared in depends_lib") was the evidence they built against branch 4.
  - **for the person:** master's openssl PortGroup knows branches `no_version 1.0 1.1 3` (`default openssl.branch {[openssl::default_branch]}`, 3), so `--set openssl.branch=4` works only on the branch that teaches the PortGroup branch 4, the openssl4 branch, whose tree the canaries build in; on master it is the evaluation's error, "Invalid OpenSSL branch: 4".
  - today `--also` builds unchanged ports against the branch: Also targets, Kind Unchanged, Role Also (engine/plan.go:122, `alsoVariants`), their directories looked up by name and added to the worktree's sparse checkout, each evaluated in each environment (`checks.PortReader.Ports`, service.go:128, the eval session's `mportopen "file://$portdir" $opts $args`, evaluator.tcl:255, with options `subport <name>` and the variants), ordered after the changed ports it depends on, and blocked in the guest where one of them didn't pass (`depends_on`). In evidence an Also target is an extra (`TargetEvidence.Extra`, evidence.go:202): shown where it ran, required nowhere, its failure accepted by name (`submit --accept`, the ExtraFailed row) and never fixed. The plan prints them on its "Also" line (view/writers.go); GitHub's workflow won't build them. A target's ID is its name and its variant spec, "jq +x11" (`model.Target.ID`, vocabulary.go:96); a build's inputs (`model.TargetInputs`, inputs.go) key the environment, directory, tree, resources, variants and active ports, and a build with identical inputs is reused. The guest (buildenv/guest/guest.tcl `build`, :911) opens the target on every port(1) line as `port -N -D <portdir> <action> subport=<name> +variant…`, the selection's `subport=<name>` itself a user option in port(1)'s syntax; it installs the dependencies `port -q echo depof:<name>` lists first (`install --unrequested`), then fetch, checksum, install from source, test; its own reads (`declares_tests`, `checkout`, `work_dir`, `portgroups`, `path_dependencies`, `links`) call `mportopen` with options `subport <name>`. In MacPorts (macports-base, read 2026-11-04) a `name=value` on port(1)'s line reaches `macports::worker_init` as `options`, which sets `user_options($opt)` and the variable before the Portfile is sourced; `handle_option` (portutil.tcl) leaves a user-set option as it is when the Portfile sets it, and `default` leaves a value already set; `options` declares each option as a Tcl command of its name, so `info commands ::openssl.branch` says whether a port declares it; PortGroup callbacks run after the Portfile and its variants and read the value in effect (the openssl group's `port:openssl<branch>` from `openssl::set_openssl_dependency`, flags, env and pkg-config paths from `openssl::configure_build`).
  - after: `--set name=value`, repeatable, on `check`, beside `--also`: each `--also` port is built with the option set as `port install libssh2 openssl.branch=4` sets it, in the check's builds alone. No Portfile is edited and no branch is made: the library branch itself is checked with its canaries, `dockhand check --also libssh2,socat,iperf3,sslscan --set openssl.branch=4`, `dockhand impact` naming the dependents to choose from. A version move goes the same way with the option its PortGroup declares, `--set python.default_version=314` for python's, perl5's and php's default-branch options alike.
  - scope: a setting applies to every `--also` port and to nothing else: not to the branch's changed ports, whose builds are what the pull request submits, and not to the dependencies the guest installs first, which install as the tree has them, from archives. `--set` without `--also` is refused: "--set applies to the ports --also names: name them, as --also libssh2,socat --set openssl.branch=4; a changed port under a setting is a line in its Portfile". On a branch that changes nothing, `--also curl --set openssl.branch=4` is an investigation, as `--also curl --variants '+gnutls +sectrust'` is (branches.md:240), ended by `archive`.
  - declared, and evaluated under the setting: each `--also` port must declare the option in the branch's tree, under its variants, the plan's evaluation asking the opened port `info commands ::openssl.branch`, as `assumed` asks for a PortGroup's options (evaluator.tcl:214). One that doesn't is refused before anything is captured: "--set openssl.branch=4: socat declares no option openssl.branch (neither its Portfile nor its PortGroups), so the setting would change nothing there; leave it out of --also, or name an option it declares". The evaluation opens the port with the setting among its options, so planning reads the port under the setting: `check --plan -v` prints its dependencies and configure.args as they are with it, a canary whose `depends_lib` becomes the branch's openssl4 is ordered after openssl4's build and blocked where it fails, and a value the PortGroup rejects is the evaluation's error, unresolved in the plan with MacPorts' words ("Invalid OpenSSL branch: 4", master's PortGroup).
  - identity: a build under settings is its own target, its ID the port's name, its variant spec, then its settings in name order, "libssh2 openssl.branch=4", "curl +gnutls openssl.branch=4", beside the port's default build as a variant build is, never in its place. Its inputs carry the settings (`TargetInputs.Settings`), so no default build's result stands for it and it stands for none; reuse is otherwise as it is.
  - the guest: the settings join the selection, `subport=libssh2 openssl.branch=4`, on every port(1) line that opens the target (clean, lint, fetch, checksum, install, test, info), and the options of every `mportopen` of it (`declares_tests`, `checkout`, `work_dir`, `portgroups`, `path_dependencies`, `links`). The dependencies it installs first are read by opening the target under its settings too (`mportopen` with the options, and `mportinfo`'s `depends_build`, `depends_lib`, `depends_run` and `depends_test`, the port of each entry its last colon field, as `depof:` resolves `path:` and `bin:` entries), since `port echo depof:` evaluates the port with no option of the person's and would list openssl3; implementation confirms that reading against `port echo depof:libssh2` on one port before relying on it. The guest's `links` then says what the canary linked, "iperf3 links openssl4's libraries, declared in depends_lib".
  - evidence and the pull request: extras, as `--also`'s are, shown where they ran, required nowhere, never evidence for the changed ports' own rows. Tested on names each by its ID, and the description gains one sentence where any build had settings: "Built against this branch with openssl.branch=4: libssh2, socat, iperf3 and sslscan (their Portfiles are unchanged)". A failure is accepted by its name, `submit --accept 'libssh2 openssl.branch=4'`; `--accept libssh2` keeps meaning the default build; status's ExtraFailed row names it the same way. The answer to the note's question: the results count as what they are, builds of unchanged ports under a setting beside the branch's own evidence; they never stand in for a changed port's build.
  - providers: Tart, local and a command provider carry `settings` per target in the guest's request (the guest's `Target` gains `settings`; the command provider's request file the same field, said in its docs). GitHub: "Won't build … on github", as for every `--also` port.
  - the plan and its JSON: the "Also" line names the builds by ID, "Also libssh2 openssl.branch=4, socat openssl.branch=4"; `--plan -v`'s evaluation block is each under its setting; `--json`'s plan gains `settings`, the options as given, beside `also`, and target IDs carry them. `--baseline` of such a check builds master's port under the same settings where it failed, as it keeps a variant build's variants (`alsoVariants`).
  - the syntax: `name=value`, the name letters, digits, dots and underscores. Refused: no `=` ("--set openssl.branch: name=value, as --set openssl.branch=4"), an empty value ("--set openssl.branch=: give its value"), a name given twice ("--set openssl.branch is given twice"). A value with spaces is the shell's to quote, `--set 'python.versions=312 313'`.
  - out of the design: a setting on a changed port (write the line; the pull request shows it), settings reaching dependencies (MacPorts propagates a user option to the dependencies it installs in one `port install`, but dockhand installs them first, as the tree has them, so a canary's mixed linkage is a finding, not a hidden rebuild), and a config default.
  - implementation notes, architecture's: `check`'s `--set` as a `StringArray`, parsed to `map[string]string` with the refusals; `engine.CheckRequest.Settings` → `PlanRequest.Settings`; where the Also candidates are made, each gets `Target.Settings`; `--set` with no `--also` refused there. `checks.PortReader.Ports` takes settings; the eval session passes them in `mportopen`'s options after `subport`; the evaluator reports each option asked for that the port doesn't declare (`info commands`), which planning turns into the refusal. `model.Target.Settings map[string]string` (`settings,omitempty`), `ID()` appending `name=value` words in name order after the variant spec; `model.Plan.Settings` as given; `TargetInputs.Settings`; additive JSON, no schema bump; `CompareTargets` orders by settings after variants. guest/follow.go's `Target` gains `Settings`; guest.tcl takes them into the selection and every `mportopen`'s options, and a `dependencies_under` read replaces `depof:` for a target with settings (or for every target, one read: implementation's call, with the comparison above). `view.WritePlan` already prints IDs; engine/body.go adds the sentence; `AcceptName` already keys by ID; branchstate unchanged.
  - Usage docs, same commit: docs/usage/checks.md, "Understand targets and test policy": the example block (line 93) gains `dockhand check --also libssh2,socat --set openssl.branch=4`, and after the `--variants` paragraphs: "`--set name=value` builds each `--also` port with a Portfile or PortGroup option set, as `port install libssh2 openssl.branch=4` sets it: a canary of the branch's library against the ports that use it, with no edit to their Portfiles and no branch of its own. It applies to the `--also` ports alone; the branch's changed ports, and the dependencies a build installs first, build as the tree has them. Each port must declare the option, in its Portfile or a PortGroup it loads, in the branch's tree: `--set openssl.branch=4` works on the branch that teaches the openssl PortGroup branch 4. Such a build is its own target, `libssh2 openssl.branch=4`, beside the port's default build and an extra as `--also`'s are: shown where it ran, required nowhere, its failure accepted by that name. Repeat `--set` for several options; `python.default_version` and the default-branch options of the perl5 and php PortGroups go the same way."; the `--plan -v` paragraph (line 21) gains "under `--set`'s options for an `--also` port"; docs/usage/publishing.md, the extras paragraph (line 260): "a build under `--set` is an extra by its name, `libssh2 openssl.branch=4`, and the description says which ports were built against the branch with which setting"; docs/usage/agents.md: `check --json`'s plan gains `settings`, and target IDs carry a build's settings after its variants; docs/usage/branches.md, the investigation paragraph (line 240): "or `check --also libssh2 --set openssl.branch=4`" beside the variants example; the command provider's docs (checks.md "Your build script", line 277, and wherever the request file's fields are listed): the `settings` field; help: `check`'s Long gains the sentence, and the flag reads `--set name=value   build each --also port with this Portfile or PortGroup option set, as port install sets name=value; repeat for several`.
  - Tests: the parse refusals; a plan with `--also` under a setting (the ID, the options the evaluation passed, the ordering after a changed dependency); the undeclared-option refusal on a fixture Portfile without the group; inputs keys differing by settings; the guest's lines with the setting (the guest's test double) and its dependency read; the body sentence; `--json`'s `plan.settings`; a baseline keeping settings; GitHub's won't-build words.
  - Acceptance: A10 runs the new checks.md and branches.md examples as their `--plan` in the stage's checkout of master, whose openssl PortGroup refuses branch 4, so it lists them not run, as naming a setting only a branch's PortGroup knows; a Tart row of a canary under a setting the stage's tree accepts is implementation's to add or decline, with its reason; run what changes with `tools/heavy tools/acceptance/quick.sh --rows "A10"`.

From the full stages and field testing before them:

- **Acceptance's H1 reads content, not refs** (the Prime-time session, rc1's full stage, 2026-10-30; low priority, at its word; the harness's). H1 reads a ref removed during a row as lost unless the row allows it, so a clean that rightly removes a ref whose changes are on upstream master reads as lost until a row names it, as A5's 2c12fa6 did on rc1, an empty commit after a merge whose changes are on master. H1 accepting a removed ref whose changes are on upstream master would spare such allowances.

Its review counted 12 missing requirements alike; only Pillow and requests broke `import mlx_vlm`, tqdm came through transformers, and the rest were for `mlx_vlm.server` or imported lazily. A requirement a dependency's own dependencies provide would count as met, which needs their evaluations in turn; and one reached only through an optional or lazy import would be said apart from one a module-level import from the package's `__init__` needs, which is reading Python's imports. Both wait behind the core.

Reading that from a repository, its archived flag or a long-quiet push with a migration notice, is a new check, so it waits behind the core. The shape where a repository's tags stop below the port's version, mergestat's, is held as ahead since v0.6.0; pomo's, whose own tag is the newest, stays here.

## Maintenance

- **Release candidates.** An rc is tagged when the M1 is free to start its acceptance, after a short window that gathers what's found since the last, so fixes found hours apart make one tag, not two (the person's "Do #3, #5, #2 & #1", 2026-10-31, item 2: rc3 followed rc2 by an hour, and part of rc2's stages were done again). Once main takes the next release's work, the release's later candidates are tagged from `release/v0.N`, made at the last candidate, each fix picked onto it as it lands on main, so the last candidate's acceptance still stands for what didn't change.
- **Acceptance rows move with the behavior they test.** A commit that changes what a person sees changes its row in the same commit, and before it lands runs those rows on the main Mac, in the heavy-run turn, with `tools/heavy tools/acceptance/quick.sh --rows "<affected>"` (the same list, item 1: rc2's quick stage lost hours to B1, B2, and A10 expecting rc1's behavior, fixed in 013d5f70). CI's acceptance self-test proves the runner and the harm sweep against a stand-in, not the rows against the build, and a lint of the rows wouldn't have caught those three; a run of the no-VM rows against main is v0.8.0's chore.
- **The main Mac's heavy runs take turns** (the same list, item 5): implementation's measures and scratch builds, the survey's runs, and field testing's checks share the Mac and its database, so they keep one rule for whose turn it is, each on its own `DOCKHAND_DB` copy with its archives beside it. The turn is `tools/heavy <command>`, a lock on `~/.dockhand-heavy.lock` that says who holds it to a run that waits; `make gate` and `make test-corpus` take it themselves (docs/development.md, "Heavy work on a shared Mac").
- **MacPorts compatibility.** Evaluator tests pass on Base 2.12.6, and CI runs them against the package of whichever release MacPorts currently points its users at, and a weekly job runs them against base's master (231f48c1). Version adapters follow the Base design prospective (2026-09-24): the current release plus a pinned master preview. Keep Base and PortGroup compatibility distinct ([evidence](macports-compatibility.md)).
- **Images.** Twelve images are made and checked: base and Xcode for macOS 12, 13, 14, 15, 26, and 27. Re-probe the facts table when an image is rebuilt; the 2026-09-27 probes found every earlier row unchanged. Golden Gate needs Tart 2.39.0 or newer.
- **Package boundaries.** Extend import checks when touching a boundary that matters; the command and engine boundary tests are the models. Don't split packages by size alone. CI runs `make deadcode`, which fails on a function nothing reaches, tests included. Keep test-only exports documented as such. After a milestone, a sweep runs the tool without `-test`: what it lists, only tests reach, and each is test support, documented as such, or dead. The tool can't see an unused exported method on a type that reaches an interface or reflection, so a sweep checks those by references too.
- **Relocations.** A batch that moves a rule to its owner lists, in its note, the call sites it switched to the owner's rule, and closes with a grep across internal/ for the old rule's shape, its predicate, sentence, or switch, deleting what it finds or saying why it stays. Each of the 5897a82 review's high findings would have been caught by that grep on the day (its section 5). A rule is moved only once every consumer carries the facts it needs and stops judging beside it: findings 3, 4, 6, and 9 of Codex's review of 8e314b96 are a consumer judging beside its owner, so a move adds an agreement test across the consumers, check, review, status, submit, ship, and serve, where they apply. The engine was 24,704 lines in 68 files at 8e314b96; it's measured again at v0.7.0's rc1, and not split for size alone.
- **Dependencies.** Minor releases go on main when they build and the gate passes, as 3706d086's did; a major version is measured first, by building main against it, and placed here like any fix (the person's "Let's go with you recommendation.", 2026-10-24 18:31Z). A new Go library is taken where it makes sense, to gain major functionality, improve performance, or eliminate logic dockhand keeps repeating (the person's "Change the dependencies rule: I'm open to new Golang libraries and dependencies where it makes sense…", 2026-10-31 23:48Z, in prioritization's thread, replacing the library survey's preference for none), weighed in the item that brings it: what it replaces, the modules it brings with it, each a go.vendors entry in dockhand's port, and its maintenance.
- **GitHub's budget.** Requests to its API are paced at 750 a minute, under its documented 900. A whole `outdated --mine` spends about 1,800 of the 5,000 an hour the person's account has, a budget shared with the GitHub CLI.
- **Test throughput.** The cost unit is the MacPorts interpreter. Keep per-test timing visible, and prefer simulated clocks for time-driven tests; v2's `workflow` tests show what wall-clock budgets do under load.
